<div class="ltx_page_content">

# Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

The growth of *agent skills* has transformed how agentic systems are
built, evaluated, and deployed. As skill libraries continue to scale,
rigorous evaluation becomes critical to ensuring their utility, quality,
and safety in real-world applications. Consequently, the field is
undergoing an emerging paradigm shift from isolated skill creation to
automated, evaluation-driven skill evolution. In this survey, we
systematically examine the landscape of skill evolution and evaluation
beyond foundational skill creation. We categorize evolution into four
distinct paradigms, spanning execution feedback, trajectory
distillation, compression, and reinforcement learning, showing how each
element contributes to improving skill utility and reliability. We also
provide an analysis of six skill-centric benchmark categories,
identifying structural gaps in benchmark coverage, trade-offs, and
metric richness to advance skill research. Finally, we identify open
directions for building skill ecosystems that are generalizable,
efficient, and verifiably safe. The project URL is
<a href="https://github.com/Cassie07/AgentSkill_Survey"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://github.com/Cassie07/AgentSkill_Survey</a>

</div>

<div id="p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<div id="p1.1" class="ltx_block ltx_align_bottom">

<span id="p1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent Skill
Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks</span>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Agent skills equip LLM agents with domain-specific knowledge at
inference time, enabling agents to perceive and interact with
environments through diverse external
tools <a href="#bib.bib80" class="ltx_ref">80</a>. Unlike prompt
engineering <a href="#bib.bib74" class="ltx_ref">74</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">5</a>, agent skills encode reusable,
portable, multi-step solutions that guide agents to address complex
tasks through coordinated decision sequences, thereby substantially
reducing tedious manual effort.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

As the scale and diversity of agent skills continue to
grow <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>, the absence of robust
evaluation frameworks has become a critical bottleneck for skill-guided
agent deployment. Meanwhile, diverse skills make the manual refinement
inherently infeasible, a challenge further compounded by the lack of
evolution approaches to capture the real-world
feedback <a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>. An outdated or
unsafe skill can propagate errors across downstream tasks, turning skill
assessment into an open problem of diagnosis, maintenance, and
alignment. It is thus essential to establish automated and continuous
mechanisms for agent skills, rather than relying on static pipelines, to
ensure skills are generalizable across tasks and verifiably safe for
public use. In this survey, we position skill evolution and evaluation
as the central focus of this emerging paradigm
(Figure <a href="#S2.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 2 What is Skill? ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>). Concretely, we introduce a
four-paradigm taxonomy of skill evolution strategies
(<a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="3 Skill Evolution ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 3</span></a>). We gain insights
into designing evolution strategies towards enhancing skill creation,
utility, and refinement with fewer human efforts. We further provide a
critical analysis of skill-centric benchmarks
(<a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="4 Skill-centric Evaluation and Benchmarks ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 4</span></a>) to assess their
opportunities in multimodal skills, trajectory distillation, and skill
security towards better real-world agent deployment.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>What is Skill?

<figure id="S2.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2606.11435/assets/Fig1_overview.png" id="S2.F1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="478"
height="113" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1: </span>We map
the landscape of agent skill evolution strategies (§3) through
comparative analysis and design recommendations. We offer evaluation
insights (§4) through structural gaps and benchmark limitations, and
open challenges (§5) for robust real-world skill
deployment.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">

An agent skill is a structured
package <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>:
$`\mathcal{S}=(C,\,\pi,\,T,\,\mathcal{R})`$, where
$`C:\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\{0,1\}`$ is the condition,
mapping the agent observation ($`\mathcal{O}`$) and goal
($`\mathcal{G}`$) to the skill relevance; $`\pi`$ is the execution
policy to encode the procedures; $`T`$ is the termination criterion,
specifying when skill execution is completed; and $`\mathcal{R}`$ is the
reusable interface to indicate the composition with other skills.

</div>

<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Human-authored skills encapsulate domain expertise as
machine-interpretable procedural
knowledge <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>. To expedite this
process, automated skill creation enables an agent to generate skills
with less human-written effort. For example, Skill
Creator <a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a> could automatically
create a full skill directory and test cases with minimal human text
description. Similarly,
Voyager <a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">67</a> creates skills as
the executable code, including proposing tasks, refining code via
environment feedback, self-verification, and updating the skill library.
To better create reusable skills, reinforcement learning (RL) is
integrated into the training loop, where rewards earned from reusing a
skill on later tasks are propagated back to update the policy. Inspired
by Group Relative Policy Optimization
(GRPO) <a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">53</a>,
SAGE <a href="#bib.bib69" class="ltx_ref">69</a> leverages the reusable
rewards from group tasks, encouraging the agent to learn and create
reusable skills. ARISE <a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>
preserves successful reasoning patterns to train agents toward
generating reusable skills, overcoming GRPO’s limitation of treating
rollouts independently.

</div>

<div id="S2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Efficient skill usage strategies involve retrieval, routing, and
management. For each task, it is common that the agent cannot load all
potential skills to assess their usability due to excessive time and
token use. To address, (a) Retrieval determines a small set of skills
from a large skill pool; (b) Routing efficiently decides which skill
should be executed at which step after retrieval; (c) Management keeps
the skills organized, up-to-date, and safe to use
(<a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix A Skill Usage ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks">Appendix <span
class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></span></a>). These usage
mechanisms establish the foundation upon which evolution and evaluation
frameworks operate as outlined below.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Skill Evolution

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Skill evolution is a continuous process to improve skill quality by
learning from past success and failure patterns to achieve up-to-date
capabilities <a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>. As the number
of skills continues to grow, scaling manual refinement becomes
increasingly impractical. This hurdle motivates automatic strategies by
leveraging skill execution records, including rich feedback signals and
task-solving trajectories <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>.
Such raw execution signals and trajectories are often noisy, mixing
successful steps with irrelevant or failed ones. Therefore, reliable
skill evolution requires capturing reusable execution patterns across
multiple trajectories rather than individual
behaviors <a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>. Moreover, growing
skill libraries likely introduce conflicting contents, leading to
redundant storage, excessive token consumption, and poor
generalizability <a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib82" class="ltx_ref">82</a>. To address these hurdles,
we outline evolution strategies especially along the source and
granularity of the learning signal: <span id="S3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Execution feedback</span> operates on
single-run step-level signals; <span id="S3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Trajectory distillation</span> operates
on multi-run sequence-level patterns; <span id="S3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Compression and augmentation</span>
operate on library-level structures; <span id="S3.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Reinforcement learning</span> operates on
task-level rewards. These paradigms are not mutually exclusive, but they
represent the dominant design choices in the community. We further
structure and analyze how the current evolution paradigms align with
benchmarks, highlighting trade-offs and practical guidelines that
motivate future research (<a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Practical Guidelines for Skill Evolution System Design ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks">Appendix <span
class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzMuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgxLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxOTQuNCAxMy42MyIgd2lkdGg9IjE5NC40Ij48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEzLjYzKSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSB0cmFuc2xhdGUoOTcuMiwwKSB0cmFuc2xhdGUoMCw2LjgyKSI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojQTY5NTg1Oy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6I0ZGRTVDQzsiIGZpbGw9IiNGRkU1Q0MiIHN0cm9rZT0iI0E2OTU4NSIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCI+PHBhdGggZD0iTSA5My42IDYuNjggTCAtOTMuNiA2LjY4IEMgLTk1LjUxIDYuNjggLTk3LjA2IDUuMTMgLTk3LjA2IDMuMjIgTCAtOTcuMDYgLTMuMjIgQyAtOTcuMDYgLTUuMTMgLTk1LjUxIC02LjY4IC05My42IC02LjY4IEwgOTMuNiAtNi42OCBDIDk1LjUxIC02LjY4IDk3LjA2IC01LjEzIDk3LjA2IC0zLjIyIEwgOTcuMDYgMy4yMiBDIDk3LjA2IDUuMTMgOTUuNTEgNi42OCA5My42IDYuNjggWiBNIC05Ny4wNiAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC05MS41MyAtMy43NikiPjxmb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0IHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1mby13aWR0aDoxMS42MWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWhlaWdodDowLjZlbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowZW07Zm9udC1zaXplOjExLjVwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iOS42MSIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgOS42MSkiIHdpZHRoPSIxODQuODIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250YWluZXIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250ZW50Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5waWMxLjEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfaW5saW5lLWJsb2NrIGZhcyBmYS1yb3RhdGUiIGFyaWEtaGlkZGVuPSJ0cnVlIj4KPC9zcGFuPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTMy5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDEucGljMS4yIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCI+4oCJRXhlY3V0aW9uIEZlZWRiYWNrPC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0PjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+"
id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The record of skill execution can reveal valuable feedback signals for
skill improvement, including runtime errors, incorrect outputs, unmet
task specifications, and execution paths. Inspired by human rewriting,
an intuitive approach is to implement an automated loop that executes
the existing skill, observes failure patterns from execution feedback,
and then rewrites the skill to prevent such failures from recurring. The
execution feedback can come from either clear
signals <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref">62</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a> or implicit execution
ones <a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>, which are both crucial for
guiding skill evolution.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Traceable signals of skill evolution can come from real engineering
activities, revealing user intent, agent tool calling, and concrete
error patterns. These signals are critical for automatically detecting,
diagnosing, and correcting flawed skills. For instance,
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillForge</span> <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>
creates new skills by detecting the discrepancies between execution and
reference behaviors. In particular, SkillForge produces structured
failure records to identify the systemic patterns, reducing the need for
human rewriting and verification. To support multi-turn conversations,
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">CoEvoSkills</span> <a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>
enables agents to reduce human–machine cognitive misalignment and
produce self-evolved skills that outperform human-curated skills.
Especially to address failed executed skills, it introduces a verifier
that can provide direct feedback about root-cause analysis and revision
suggestions. Accessing the rich environment feedback can further enhance
skill reliability. <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">EmbodiSkill</span> <a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>
leverages the agent execution feedback by interacting with the
environment to produce a trajectory of actions, observations, and a
final reward. Unlike relying on the real execution feedback,
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Skills-Coach</span> <a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref">62</a>
executes skills on synthetic cases to achieve the evolution feedback.
Skills-Coach produces several rewritten versions of the seed skill. The
highest-scored rewritten skill serves as a successful signal to improve
skill instructions, while the failure traces drive skill scripts to
prevent failures. Similarly, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Ctx2Skill</span> <a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>
learns from feedbacks by producing synthetic diagnostic questions from
the reference document.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Even without a clear execution signal, user’s preferences across
conversations, such as preferred tone, terminology, or writing
conventions, remain valuable for improving skill evolution. We support
that the interaction traces could contain rich signals with reusable
knowledge. <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AutoSkill</span> <a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>
treats interactions from users as the main signal for skill evolution.
Rather than relying solely on failure correction, it turns user
preferences into explicit capabilities that personalize the agent’s
behavior. Similarly, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillClaw</span> <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>
leverages heterogeneous user experiences from key trajectories that
reflect how different users interact with tools and workflows.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4" class="ltx_para">

Across above execution feedback studies, we identify that a structured
failure mode becomes a meaningful design factor. Systems that well
separate failure diagnosis from rewrite generation
(<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillForge</span> <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>,
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">CoEvoSkills</span> <a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>)
tend to report stronger cross-task results compared to systems operating
on raw traces (<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AutoSkill</span> <a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>,
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillClaw</span> <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>),
although head-to-head comparisons remain absent. It is evident that
feedback signals are inherently bounded by execution environment
diversity rather than deployment—a structural constraint worth explicit
attention in design and evaluation of skills.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzMuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgyLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAyMDYuODYgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIyMDYuODYiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxMDMuNDMsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6IzhEOTg4OTstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNEOUVBRDM7IiBmaWxsPSIjRDlFQUQzIiBzdHJva2U9IiM4RDk4ODkiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gOTkuODMgNi42OCBMIC05OS44MyA2LjY4IEMgLTEwMS43NCA2LjY4IC0xMDMuMjkgNS4xMyAtMTAzLjI5IDMuMjIgTCAtMTAzLjI5IC0zLjIyIEMgLTEwMy4yOSAtNS4xMyAtMTAxLjc0IC02LjY4IC05OS44MyAtNi42OCBMIDk5LjgzIC02LjY4IEMgMTAxLjc0IC02LjY4IDEwMy4yOSAtNS4xMyAxMDMuMjkgLTMuMjIgTCAxMDMuMjkgMy4yMiBDIDEwMy4yOSA1LjEzIDEwMS43NCA2LjY4IDk5LjgzIDYuNjggWiBNIC0xMDMuMjkgLTYuNjgiIC8+PC9nPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCAtOTcuNzYgLTMuNzYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MTIuMzFlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6MC42ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8tZGVwdGg6MC4xN2VtO2ZvbnQtc2l6ZToxMS41cHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEyLjMiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDkuNjEpIiB3aWR0aD0iMTk1Ljk2Ij48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTMy5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDIucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBmYXMgZmEtcm91dGUiIGFyaWEtaGlkZGVuPSJ0cnVlIj4KPC9zcGFuPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTMy5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDIucGljMS4yIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCI+4oCJVHJhamVjdG9yeSBEaXN0aWxsYXRpb248L3NwYW4+PC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Q+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4="
id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Skill evolution via trajectory distillation gains momentum to improve
skills through sequential memorization by capturing task-specific,
reusable patterns. For instance, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SPARK</span> <a href="#bib.bib88" class="ltx_ref">88</a>
explores online trajectory verification to distill strong skills from
executable evidence. It introduces a key trajectory-level measure to
assess skill performance using task-environment evidence rather than
unverified prior plans. <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Trace2Skill</span> <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>
updates skills from multiple execution trajectories by generating
targeted patches from success and failure cases, then merging redundant
fixes into a single conflict-free skill file.
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Memento-Skills</span> <a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref">86</a>
introduces a read-write reflective loop for skill evolution: a router
retrieves relevant skills for execution, and the agent updates them from
execution feedback, enabling iterative refinement and long-term
behavioral memory. To broaden the data modality,
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">XSkill</span> <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>
grounds skill learning in visual observations, capturing relations
between visual states and decisions. It extracts task-level skills
through visual summarization and action-level experiences through
cross-rollout critiques of successes and failures, consolidating both
into a unified skill bank through merging and refinement.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzMuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgzLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAzMDEuODIgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIzMDEuODIiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxNTAuOTEsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6Izg3OTM5RTstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNDRkUyRjM7IiBmaWxsPSIjQ0ZFMkYzIiBzdHJva2U9IiM4NzkzOUUiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTQ3LjMxIDYuNjggTCAtMTQ3LjMxIDYuNjggQyAtMTQ5LjIyIDYuNjggLTE1MC43NyA1LjEzIC0xNTAuNzcgMy4yMiBMIC0xNTAuNzcgLTMuMjIgQyAtMTUwLjc3IC01LjEzIC0xNDkuMjIgLTYuNjggLTE0Ny4zMSAtNi42OCBMIDE0Ny4zMSAtNi42OCBDIDE0OS4yMiAtNi42OCAxNTAuNzcgLTUuMTMgMTUwLjc3IC0zLjIyIEwgMTUwLjc3IDMuMjIgQyAxNTAuNzcgNS4xMyAxNDkuMjIgNi42OCAxNDcuMzEgNi42OCBaIE0gLTE1MC43NyAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC0xNDUuMjQgLTMuNzYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MTguMjhlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6MC42ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8tZGVwdGg6MC4xN2VtO2ZvbnQtc2l6ZToxMS41cHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEyLjMiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDkuNjEpIiB3aWR0aD0iMjkwLjkyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTMy5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDMucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBmYXMgZmEtY29tcHJlc3MiIGFyaWEtaGlkZGVuPSJ0cnVlIj4KPC9zcGFuPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTMy5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDMucGljMS4yIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCI+4oCJQ29tcHJlc3Npb24gYW5kIEF1Z21lbnRhdGlvbjwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9zcGFuPjwvZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdD48L2c+PC9nPjwvc3ZnPg=="
id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

As skill libraries grow rapidly, skill overlap and conflict lead to
redundant exploration and poor
generalization <a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>. Therefore, skill
compression and augmentation are increasingly important for reducing
duplication, gaining complementary knowledge, and exploring reliable
skills. <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillNet</span> <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>
enables the community to create, evaluate, and connect agent skills as
sourced from GitHub repositories and office documents. The resulting
skill-similarity relation graph becomes a key to indicate whether the
existing skills should be reused or merged. However, SkillNet does not
explicitly define skill categories to support its evolution. To address
this, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillX</span> <a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>
builds a multi-category skill evolution derived from execution
trajectories by merging similar skills, decomposing complex ones, and
assessing their generalization. To broaden coverage, SkillX prioritizes
underexplored or failure-prone tools and synthesizes novel tasks to
acquire and validate new skills to improve skill quality, richness, and
coverage. <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillFoundry</span> <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">54</a>
takes a knowledge-driven approach for skill augmentation, organizing a
tree structure where each node tracks references and existing skills to
prioritize underexplored branches. This structure mines heterogeneous
scientific resources into executable skills. Alternatively,
<span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillReducer</span> <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>
reframes skill evolution as content cleanup, pruning overly long skill
descriptions and reorganizing skills into actionable rules and
references, preserving essential knowledge while reducing token
overhead. As these skills move into deployment, a promising frontier is
grounding compression and augmentation decisions in live signals such as
retrieval frequency and runtime failure rates rather than the offline
design alone.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzMuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg0LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAyMjguMjEgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIyMjguMjEiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxMTQuMTEsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6Izk2OEQ5RTstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNFNkQ5RjM7IiBmaWxsPSIjRTZEOUYzIiBzdHJva2U9IiM5NjhEOUUiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTEwLjUxIDYuNjggTCAtMTEwLjUxIDYuNjggQyAtMTEyLjQyIDYuNjggLTExMy45NyA1LjEzIC0xMTMuOTcgMy4yMiBMIC0xMTMuOTcgLTMuMjIgQyAtMTEzLjk3IC01LjEzIC0xMTIuNDIgLTYuNjggLTExMC41MSAtNi42OCBMIDExMC41MSAtNi42OCBDIDExMi40MiAtNi42OCAxMTMuOTcgLTUuMTMgMTEzLjk3IC0zLjIyIEwgMTEzLjk3IDMuMjIgQyAxMTMuOTcgNS4xMyAxMTIuNDIgNi42OCAxMTAuNTEgNi42OCBaIE0gLTExMy45NyAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC0xMDguNDMgLTMuNzYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MTMuNjZlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6MC42ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8tZGVwdGg6MC4xN2VtO2ZvbnQtc2l6ZToxMS41cHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEyLjMiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDkuNjEpIiB3aWR0aD0iMjE3LjMxIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTMy5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDQucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBmYXMgZmEtdHJvcGh5IiBhcmlhLWhpZGRlbj0idHJ1ZSI+Cjwvc3Bhbj48c3BhbiBpZD0iUzMuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg0LnBpYzEuMiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X2JvbGQiPuKAiVJlaW5mb3JjZW1lbnQgTGVhcm5pbmc8L3NwYW4+PC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Q+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4="
id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px4.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Reinforcement learning (RL) <a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib89" class="ltx_ref">89</a> has emerged as a principled
approach for aligning LLM agents with task execution rewards and driving
reliable skill evolution <a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">67</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib73" class="ltx_ref">73</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">20</a>. However, standard RL only
rewards single task per update, while the real value of a skill roots in
its reusability across
tasks <a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>. To achieve stable
rewards, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">D2Skill</span> <a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>
leverages the multiple-rollout strategy in GRPO, enabling the policy
agent to generate highly reusable skills. For each task, the LLM agent
retrieves the most relevant skills and runs them twice (with the
retrieved skills and without). The resulting success-rate gap between
the two rollouts yields more stable rewards and improved skill
reusability. Also, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillRL</span> <a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">76</a>
leverages GRPO and exploits both success and failure signals, collecting
trajectories across multiple rollouts to update the policy for skill
retrieval and refinement by identifying failure patterns to drive
targeted skill revision. However, these
studies <a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">76</a> treat skill retrieval,
utilization, and evolution as separate components, risking conflicts
during concurrent updates. <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Skill1</span> <a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">55</a>
overcomes this fragmentation via a unified co-evolution by training a
single policy to jointly perform skill search, selection, task solving,
and skill evolution within a single rollout. Overall, we observe that
major efforts trace a clear trajectory in RL-based skill evolution.
These approaches rely on task-level rewards that can conflate skill
quality with agent capability, leaving open whether performance gains
come from skill evolution or model improvement.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Skill-centric Evaluation and Benchmarks

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Evaluation is important in the agent skill lifecycle because skills are
continuously created, evolved, and shared among users. Without rigorous
evaluation, it is difficult to fairly assess skill quality and safety.
In principle, the skill evaluation should serve as: (1) validation of
the comprehensive task performance; (2) comparison among skills in a
fair environment; (3) safety auditing to detect harmful behaviors before
skill deployments. We discuss skill-centric benchmarks to measure the
realistic performance of agent skills. In
<a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 4 Skill-centric Evaluation and Benchmarks ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table 2</span></a>, we group them into six
major categories. We also include general-domain benchmarks that were
not designed for skill evaluation yet still applicable to assess agent
skills (details in <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix B General-domain Benchmarks ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks">Appendix <span
class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 4 Skill-centric Evaluation and Benchmarks ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table 2</span></a>reveals three structural
gaps that warrant systematic investigation. First, utility and safety
benchmarks collectively cover 11 professional domains and 581 auditable
packages, whereas generation benchmarks span only 15 sub-domains with 20
core tasks. Second, no existing benchmark evaluates evolution
longitudinally, i.e., tracking whether a skill improves across multiple
rounds of feedback rather than measuring a single snapshot. Third,
evaluation metrics are predominantly binary (pass/fail), which overlook
operational factors such as token cost, latency, and error type. These
gaps should guide future research as much as the benchmarks themselves.

</div>

<tr id="S4.T2.1.5" class="ltx_tr" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFFACD;">
<td colspan="3" id="S4.T2.1.5.1"
class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_align_middle ltx_border_t"

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgxLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAyMzUuNzQgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIyMzUuNzQiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxMTcuODcsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6Izk2OUNBNjstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNFNkYwRkY7IiBmaWxsPSIjRTZGMEZGIiBzdHJva2U9IiM5NjlDQTYiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTE0LjI3IDYuNjggTCAtMTE0LjI3IDYuNjggQyAtMTE2LjE4IDYuNjggLTExNy43MyA1LjEzIC0xMTcuNzMgMy4yMiBMIC0xMTcuNzMgLTMuMjIgQyAtMTE3LjczIC01LjEzIC0xMTYuMTggLTYuNjggLTExNC4yNyAtNi42OCBMIDExNC4yNyAtNi42OCBDIDExNi4xOCAtNi42OCAxMTcuNzMgLTUuMTMgMTE3LjczIC0zLjIyIEwgMTE3LjczIDMuMjIgQyAxMTcuNzMgNS4xMyAxMTYuMTggNi42OCAxMTQuMjcgNi42OCBaIE0gLTExNy43MyAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC0xMTIuMiAtMy43NikiPjxmb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0IHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1mby13aWR0aDoxNC4xNmVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWhlaWdodDowLjZlbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowLjE3ZW07Zm9udC1zaXplOjExLjVwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iMTIuMyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgOS42MSkiIHdpZHRoPSIyMjUuMjgiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250YWluZXIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250ZW50Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5waWMxLjEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfaW5saW5lLWJsb2NrIGZhcyBmYS13cmVuY2giIGFyaWEtaGlkZGVuPSJ0cnVlIj4KPC9zcGFuPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDEucGljMS4yIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCI+4oCJU2tpbGwgVXRpbGl0eSBCZW5jaG1hcmtzPC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0PjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+"
id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Skill utility remains the primary criterion for assessing how skills
improve an agent’s task completion
performance <a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>. <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillsBench</span> <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>
covers 86 hand-built tasks across 11 professional domains with
deterministic verifiers. Across 7,308 trajectories and 7 agent-model
configurations, curated skills raise the average 16% pass rate with
effects ranging from $`+4.5`$ pp in software engineering to $`+51.9`$ pp
in healthcare. SkillsBench has since become a source for following
benchmarks that extend the evaluation scope. For instance,
SkillTester <a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">70</a> jointly measures
utility and safety and
SkillRouter <a href="#bib.bib84" class="ltx_ref">84</a> evaluates skill
retrieval and reranking. Alternatively,
<span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillCraft</span> <a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">8</a>
focuses on identifying whether agents can reuse their own tool
compositions across tasks. In particular, SkillCraft introduces
long-horizon tool-use tasks whose difficulty is scaled along two axes: a
quantitative axis that increases the number of agent-process entries,
and a structural axis that lengthens the tool-call chain by composing
subtasks into deeper workflows. Under its protocol, a compelling trait
is that an agent can package the successful tool sequence as a reusable
skill for later tasks. To date, the tool-calling cost, latency, and
reasoning quality remain underexplored factors that future benchmarks
should explicitly address.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgyLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAyNzMuMjIgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIyNzMuMjIiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxMzYuNjEsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6I0E2QTM4NTstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNGRkZBQ0Q7IiBmaWxsPSIjRkZGQUNEIiBzdHJva2U9IiNBNkEzODUiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTMzLjAxIDYuNjggTCAtMTMzLjAxIDYuNjggQyAtMTM0LjkyIDYuNjggLTEzNi40NyA1LjEzIC0xMzYuNDcgMy4yMiBMIC0xMzYuNDcgLTMuMjIgQyAtMTM2LjQ3IC01LjEzIC0xMzQuOTIgLTYuNjggLTEzMy4wMSAtNi42OCBMIDEzMy4wMSAtNi42OCBDIDEzNC45MiAtNi42OCAxMzYuNDcgLTUuMTMgMTM2LjQ3IC0zLjIyIEwgMTM2LjQ3IDMuMjIgQyAxMzYuNDcgNS4xMyAxMzQuOTIgNi42OCAxMzMuMDEgNi42OCBaIE0gLTEzNi40NyAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC0xMzAuOTQgLTMuNzYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MTYuNDllbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6MC42ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8tZGVwdGg6MC4xN2VtO2ZvbnQtc2l6ZToxMS41cHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEyLjMiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDkuNjEpIiB3aWR0aD0iMjYyLjMyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDIucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBmYXMgZmEtc2VlZGxpbmciIGFyaWEtaGlkZGVuPSJ0cnVlIj4KPC9zcGFuPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDIucGljMS4yIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCI+4oCJU2tpbGwgR2VuZXJhdGlvbiBCZW5jaG1hcmtzPC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0PjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+"
id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px2.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The skill generation benchmarks provide a quantitative evaluation of
skill quality, which is increasingly critical as manual quality
assessment becomes challenging nowadays. As hundreds of skills are
produced, early identification of low-quality skills could reduce token
consumption. <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillLearnBench</span> <a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref">85</a>
contains 20 skill-dependent tasks across 15 sub-domains, each with
multiple variants that have the same task structure with different input
values, enabling evaluation of skill reuse beyond one-shot success.
SkillLearnBench indicates that external execution feedback is essential
for skill improvement by preventing error accumulation. To better
evaluate skill generation, research efforts should assess skill document
quality, adherence between execution and prescribed steps, and task
completion success.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgzLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAzMjYuMDUgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIzMjYuMDUiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxNjMuMDMsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6I0EzOTk4QzstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNGQUVCRDc7IiBmaWxsPSIjRkFFQkQ3IiBzdHJva2U9IiNBMzk5OEMiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTU5LjQzIDYuNjggTCAtMTU5LjQzIDYuNjggQyAtMTYxLjM0IDYuNjggLTE2Mi44OSA1LjEzIC0xNjIuODkgMy4yMiBMIC0xNjIuODkgLTMuMjIgQyAtMTYyLjg5IC01LjEzIC0xNjEuMzQgLTYuNjggLTE1OS40MyAtNi42OCBMIDE1OS40MyAtNi42OCBDIDE2MS4zNCAtNi42OCAxNjIuODkgLTUuMTMgMTYyLjg5IC0zLjIyIEwgMTYyLjg5IDMuMjIgQyAxNjIuODkgNS4xMyAxNjEuMzQgNi42OCAxNTkuNDMgNi42OCBaIE0gLTE2Mi44OSAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC0xNTcuMzUgLTMuNzYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MTkuODZlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6MC42ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8tZGVwdGg6MC4xN2VtO2ZvbnQtc2l6ZToxMS41cHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEyLjMiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDkuNjEpIiB3aWR0aD0iMzE2LjA0Ij48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDMucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBmYXMgZmEtc2VhcmNoIiBhcmlhLWhpZGRlbj0idHJ1ZSI+Cjwvc3Bhbj48c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgzLnBpYzEuMiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X2JvbGQiPuKAiVNraWxsIFJldHJpZXZhbCAmYW1wOyBSb3V0aW5nIEJlbmNobWFya3M8L3NwYW4+PC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Q+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4="
id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px3.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Skill routing and retrieval benchmark focuses on evaluating the
effectiveness and accuracy of skill usage. Effective skill-retrieval
methods must distinguish near-duplicate skills, while a well-designed
routing should coordinate the appropriate skills for each task.
<span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillRouter</span> <a href="#bib.bib84" class="ltx_ref">84</a>
contains roughly 80K skills and 75 expert-verified queries. SkillRouter
demonstrates that only skill names and descriptions could result in a
31-44% drop in routing accuracy compared to using the full skill body.
<span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SRA-Bench</span> <a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">59</a>
decomposes skill augmentation into three separate stages: retrieval,
incorporation, and application. It mixes 636 manually written gold
skills into a 26,262-skill web-collected corpus and pairs them with
5,400 capability-intensive instances drawn from TheoremQA, LogicBench,
ToolQA, MedCalc-Bench, CHAMP, and BigCodeBench.
<span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AgentSkillOS
Benchmark</span> <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a> shifts the
focus of evaluation to the orchestration that allows multiple skills to
work together on a task. It constructs 30 artifact-rich tasks across
data computation, document creation, visual design, and web interaction.
Evaluating at ecosystem scales from 200 to 200K skills, this
orchestration substantially outperforms single-skill approaches when
using the same skill set.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg0LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAyNzcuODEgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIyNzcuODEiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxMzguOTEsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6IzlDOTJBMzstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNGMEUxRkE7IiBmaWxsPSIjRjBFMUZBIiBzdHJva2U9IiM5QzkyQTMiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTM1LjMxIDYuNjggTCAtMTM1LjMxIDYuNjggQyAtMTM3LjIyIDYuNjggLTEzOC43NyA1LjEzIC0xMzguNzcgMy4yMiBMIC0xMzguNzcgLTMuMjIgQyAtMTM4Ljc3IC01LjEzIC0xMzcuMjIgLTYuNjggLTEzNS4zMSAtNi42OCBMIDEzNS4zMSAtNi42OCBDIDEzNy4yMiAtNi42OCAxMzguNzcgLTUuMTMgMTM4Ljc3IC0zLjIyIEwgMTM4Ljc3IDMuMjIgQyAxMzguNzcgNS4xMyAxMzcuMjIgNi42OCAxMzUuMzEgNi42OCBaIE0gLTEzOC43NyAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC0xMzMuMjMgLTMuNzYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MTYuOGVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWhlaWdodDowLjZlbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowLjE3ZW07Zm9udC1zaXplOjExLjVwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iMTIuMyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgOS42MSkiIHdpZHRoPSIyNjcuMzYiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250YWluZXIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250ZW50Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4NC5waWMxLjEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfaW5saW5lLWJsb2NrIGZhcyBmYS1sb2NrIiBhcmlhLWhpZGRlbj0idHJ1ZSI+Cjwvc3Bhbj48c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg0LnBpYzEuMiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X2JvbGQiPuKAiVNraWxsIFNhZmV0eSBBdWRpdGluZyBCZW5jaG1hcmtzPC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0PjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+"
id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px4.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Skill security threats become increasingly systemic as malicious skills
can compromise user data, hijack execution flows, and silently degrade
agent behavior. <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillTester</span> <a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">70</a>
jointly assesses utility and safety by executing candidate skills,
reporting a composite security score alongside a three-level status
label (e.g., safe, warning, or unsafe) that allows users to weigh
performance gains against known risks. <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SkillGuardBench</span> <a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>
treats each skill as a multi-file package labeled as benign, suspicious,
or malicious. The benchmark is built upon 581 packages across five
evaluation views, with risk samples covering three recurring attack
patterns: hidden override, disguised transfer, and remote bootstrap. In
addition to auditing skills, <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SKILL-INJECT</span> <a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>
shifts the threat model towards runtime vulnerability, testing whether
agents will execute malicious instructions embedded inside otherwise
legitimate skills. Although safety auditing benchmarks have advanced,
they treat safety as a one-time gate, leaving post-installation skill
behavior across evolving libraries largely unexamined.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg1LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAzMjcuMjcgMTMuNjMiIHdpZHRoPSIzMjcuMjciPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuNjMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgxNjMuNjQsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6Izk2OUY5MjstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNFNkY1RTE7IiBmaWxsPSIjRTZGNUUxIiBzdHJva2U9IiM5NjlGOTIiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTYwLjA0IDYuNjggTCAtMTYwLjA0IDYuNjggQyAtMTYxLjk1IDYuNjggLTE2My41IDUuMTMgLTE2My41IDMuMjIgTCAtMTYzLjUgLTMuMjIgQyAtMTYzLjUgLTUuMTMgLTE2MS45NSAtNi42OCAtMTYwLjA0IC02LjY4IEwgMTYwLjA0IC02LjY4IEMgMTYxLjk1IC02LjY4IDE2My41IC01LjEzIDE2My41IC0zLjIyIEwgMTYzLjUgMy4yMiBDIDE2My41IDUuMTMgMTYxLjk1IDYuNjggMTYwLjA0IDYuNjggWiBNIC0xNjMuNSAtNi42OCIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjJwdCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIC0xNTcuOTYgLTMuNzYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MTkuOTRlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6MC42ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8tZGVwdGg6MC4xN2VtO2ZvbnQtc2l6ZToxMS41cHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEyLjMiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDkuNjEpIiB3aWR0aD0iMzE3LjI1Ij48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDUucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBmYXMgZmEtbGFwdG9wLWNvZGUiIGFyaWEtaGlkZGVuPSJ0cnVlIj4KPC9zcGFuPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzAuU1NTMC5QeDUucGljMS4yIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCI+4oCJU29mdHdhcmUgRW5naW5lZXJpbmcgQmVuY2htYXJrPC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0PjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+"
id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px5.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

The growth of skills in software engineering demands more rigorous
benchmarking. Current efforts are strongly tied to public repositories,
leaving the real-world engineering use
unexplored. <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">SWE-Skills-Bench</span> <a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">15</a>
takes a meaningful step toward closing this gap by pairing 49 publicly
available software-engineering skills with GitHub repositories and
evaluating them across 565 automated task instances. Its reliance on
publicly curated skills and fixed-commit repositories allows good
reproducibility; however, it inherently omits proprietary workflows,
legacy codebases, and continuously evolving engineering practices.
Future benchmarks should shift towards this trend by incorporating
industry-partnered task suites, dynamic repository states, and
evaluation metrics that capture maintenance overhead, error recovery
rate, and token efficiency. Meanwhile, stratifying skills by authorship
expertise offers an opportunity to advance the field from
implementation-level evaluation toward architecture-level understanding.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px6" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### <span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg2LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy42MyIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMy4wNXB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAzMTYuNCAxMy42MyIgd2lkdGg9IjMxNi40Ij48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEzLjYzKSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSB0cmFuc2xhdGUoMTU4LjIsMCkgdHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsNi44MikiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6I0E2OTY5NjstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNGRkU2RTY7IiBmaWxsPSIjRkZFNkU2IiBzdHJva2U9IiNBNjk2OTYiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiPjxwYXRoIGQ9Ik0gMTU0LjYgNi42OCBMIC0xNTQuNiA2LjY4IEMgLTE1Ni41MiA2LjY4IC0xNTguMDYgNS4xMyAtMTU4LjA2IDMuMjIgTCAtMTU4LjA2IC0zLjIyIEMgLTE1OC4wNiAtNS4xMyAtMTU2LjUyIC02LjY4IC0xNTQuNiAtNi42OCBMIDE1NC42IC02LjY4IEMgMTU2LjUyIC02LjY4IDE1OC4wNiAtNS4xMyAxNTguMDYgLTMuMjIgTCAxNTguMDYgMy4yMiBDIDE1OC4wNiA1LjEzIDE1Ni41MiA2LjY4IDE1NC42IDYuNjggWiBNIC0xNTguMDYgLTYuNjgiIC8+PC9nPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC4ycHQiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCAtMTUyLjUzIC0zLjc2KSI+PGZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Qgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZvLXdpZHRoOjE5LjM0ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8taGVpZ2h0OjAuNmVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjAuMTdlbTtmb250LXNpemU6MTEuNXB0OyIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMi4zIiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCA5LjYxKSIgd2lkdGg9IjMwNy43MSI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg2LnBpYzEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9pbmxpbmUtYmxvY2sgZmFzIGZhLWdsb2JlIiBhcmlhLWhpZGRlbj0idHJ1ZSI+Cjwvc3Bhbj48c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHg2LnBpYzEuMiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X2JvbGQiPuKAiVJlYWwtV29ybGQgRW52aXJvbm1lbnQgQmVuY2htYXJrczwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9zcGFuPjwvZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdD48L2c+PC9nPjwvc3ZnPg=="
id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px6.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1" class="ltx_para">

Evaluating agent skills in real-world environments is difficult to
standardize, but it is essential because only dynamic, open-ended
settings can reveal true deployment readiness of
skills. <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">WildClawBench</span> <a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">9</a>
drops agents into a live personal-assistant environment and runs 60
hand-built original tasks across productivity flow, code intelligence,
social interaction, and safety alignment. Users can submit results from
their own customized agents, turning the benchmark into a
community-driven testbed for skill ecosystems. Similarly,
SkillForge <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a> introduces a
benchmark of five real-world cloud technical-support scenarios spanning
1,883 tickets and 3,737 tasks. While both benchmarks advance the
real-world evaluation, their closed-environment designs restrict
external reproducibility and cross-method comparison. Moving forward,
the field would benefit from open skill execution environments with
standardized task interfaces, paired with auditable abilities of skill
library updates.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Reflection and Future Directions

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Evaluation and evolution are becoming cornerstones of trustworthy agent
skills. A central question is how to transform heterogeneous
experiences, including human-written instructions, execution traces,
user feedback, tool calls, and multimodal observations, into reliable
and verifiable knowledge. Despite recent progress, open challenges
persist in the handling of multimodal skills, effective use of
trajectory data, and skill security, all of which demand systematic
research efforts into robust evolution and evaluation frameworks.

</div>

<div id="S5.p2" class="ltx_para">

Major skill frameworks use text-centered procedural packages that work
for language, code, document, and API tasks, yet are ill-suited for
agents operating in multimodal environments. Rich multimodal examples
include desktop interfaces, web pages, embodied simulators, robotics,
medical images, and visually grounded scientific
workflows <a href="#bib.bib91" class="ltx_ref">91</a>. In these
scenarios, the right agent action is not from a textual goal alone; it
also highly depends on the visual state, spatial layout, object
configuration, interface affordances, and modality-specific
constraints <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>. A multimodal
skill should identify the target visual elements, interpret the current
state, and map it back to the content described in the skill.
XSkill <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a> offers insights into
action-level, context-specific tool selection, and its skills capture
task-level procedural knowledge for planning. Skill retrieval is driven
by visual observations, which are revealed in past trajectories. In
physical embodied settings, skill evolution could be driven by the
execution feedback from environmental interactions, which reflects the
agent’s actions, observations, and
rewards <a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>. Yet the design,
evaluation, and sharing of cross-modality skills that enable agents to
act across diverse real-world sensory inputs remain largely
underexplored.

</div>

<div id="S5.p3" class="ltx_para">

Trajectory data records <a href="#bib.bib88" class="ltx_ref">88</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a> are crucial for broadening
agent skill utility by revealing intermediate reasoning, tool choices,
recovery attempts, and failure modes. However, raw trajectories are
often redundant and noisy. A successful trace can contain irrelevant
steps, while a failed one may still hold useful local
decisions <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>. To build robust
agent skills, two emerging designs could make knowledge distillation
from trajectory data more effective. First, distillation operates over
batches of trajectories rather than single runs, since comparing many
traces against each other is what isolates reusable patterns from
task-specific noise <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>. Second,
distillation can be continuous rather than one-shot, with skills serving
as an evolving memory that is iteratively updated to reflect
environmental changes <a href="#bib.bib88" class="ltx_ref">88</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref">86</a>. To unlock the full value of
trajectory data, explicit curation along quality, diversity, and
difficulty dimensions is essential to the skill evolution.

</div>

<div id="S5.p4" class="ltx_para">

Agent skills introduce non-trivial security risks that warrant
systematic evaluation <a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">70</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>. Malicious skills could
manipulate LLMs to leak sensitive data, execute unauthorized commands,
or produce harmful decisions. We identify three principal sources of
skill poisoning that should be detected early and strictly avoided
during skill evolution. First, direct instruction poisoning embeds
harmful instructions into the skill, causing unsafe behavior during
execution. Second, prompt injection occurs when a benign skill pulls
content from untrusted external sources that carry malicious
instructions. Third, uncontrolled skill self-evolution can silently
strip existing safety constraints through unregulated updates. Further,
skills distilled from execution trajectories risk unintentional privacy
leakage through the skill body or outputs. Such risks are particularly
acute in safety-critical domains such as healthcare and finance, where
human oversight should be mandatory before skill deployment. To avoid
such skill poisoning, we believe that a robust defense requires
multi-layered approaches: a) establishing public reputation systems that
track skill authorship, b) enforcing fine-grained permission boundaries
on skill scripts, and c) requiring explicit user confirmation before
skills trigger sensitive actions.

</div>

<div id="S5.p5" class="ltx_para">

Current evolution and evaluation strategies are largely treated as
sequential, decoupled stages: a skill is evaluated, judged, and then
evolved, after which evaluation restarts from scratch. This pipeline
assumption is increasingly untenable at scale since the cost of
re-evaluating an entire library after each evolution cycle becomes
prohibitive. Two emerging approaches begin to close this gap from a
joint learning perspective.
SkillOS <a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a> builds an
experience-driven RL recipe that pairs a frozen executor with a
trainable skill curator using grouped task streams. Earlier trajectories
update the skill repository, while later related tasks immediately
evaluate those updates, leading to the effective evaluation via a
structural component of the training loop rather than an external judge.
Meanwhile, SkillsVote <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>
approaches the same problem through a lifecycle governance, profiling a
million-scale corpus for quality and verifiability. It attributes
post-execution outcomes to skill use versus environment signals, and
admits only successful discoveries through evidence-gated updates,
showing that governed skill libraries can improve frozen agents without
any model updates.

</div>

<div id="S5.p6" class="ltx_para">

Realizing a unified framework that operates over living skill libraries
more broadly demands meaningful progress on three key fronts. First,
skill libraries must be engineered with explicit versioning and
dependency graphs, such that a localized update to one skill can be
automatically tested for downstream effects as evidenced in
SkillsVote <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a> and
SkillX <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>. Second, evaluation
signals should move beyond binary pass/fail toward composite rewards
that capture latency, token cost, and generalizability. SkillOS’s
composite reward design and SkillReducer’s token-efficiency setting
begin to address this
challenge <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>. Third, skill
curators must generalize across executor backbones and task domains.
SkillOS <a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a> shows empirically
across multi-turn and single-turn reasoning settings, yet no existing
benchmark treats cross-domain curator generalization as an evaluation
target. Together, these directions suggest redesigning skill libraries
from static repositories into a more living, monitored infrastructure,
where evolution and evaluation can be two faces of the shared continuous
learning process <a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">12</a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

In the era of rapid skills growth, the ability to continuously evolve
and rigorously evaluate skills becomes vital to trustworthy agent
deployment. We introduce a four-class taxonomy of skill evolution
strategies, revealing that each class operates on distinct signal
sources with performance trade-offs. Meanwhile, we analyze six
skill-centric benchmark categories to thoroughly assess existing skills
for public use. We identify structural gaps in longitudinal evaluation,
retrieval coverage, and metric richness that should guide the next
generation of benchmarks. Finally, future research should treat skill
ecosystems as evolving infrastructure, where continual evaluation and
evolution are central for reliable use, dependency control, and
real-world agent deployment.

</div>

</div>

<div id="Sx1" class="section ltx_section">

## Limitations

<div id="Sx1.p1" class="ltx_para">

This survey provides an overview of agent skill evolution and evaluation
with several limitations. First, given the rapid development of agent
skill research, some recent methods or benchmarks may not be fully
covered. Second, the reviewed systems are evaluated on different
benchmarks and base models; we report findings as described rather than
providing a unified empirical comparison, which is better addressed by a
dedicated benchmarking study. Finally, we draw primarily on published
papers and public repositories, and may therefore understate industrial
practices where implementation details and proprietary skills remain
undisclosed.

</div>

</div>

<div id="Sx2" class="section ltx_section">

## Ethical Considerations

<div id="Sx2.p1" class="ltx_para">

AI assistant was used to refine the appendix table (i.e.,
<a href="#A2.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 3 ‣ Mathematical Reasoning. ‣ Appendix B General-domain Benchmarks ‣ Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks">Appendix <span
class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></span></a>). All technical
contents and final manuscript materials were reviewed and verified by
the authors.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Skill Usage

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Skills are indexed by their name and description for rapid retrieval,
while the full content will be loaded only upon selection. Yet
SkillRouter <a href="#bib.bib84" class="ltx_ref">84</a> finds that skill
names and descriptions alone are inaccurate for skill selection at
scale. Instead, SkillRouter adopts a retriever and a reranker to
determine candidate skills by using full skill content. To reduce the
cost of skill retrieval,
SkillFlow <a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a> avoids repeating
skill retrieval by first identifying the missing skill required to solve
the task, then querying an external agent for the successfully executed
skill, and saving it locally for future use. Unlike retrieval, effective
routing enables the LLM agent to coordinate the appropriate skill to a
specific task.
SkillOrchestra <a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref">68</a> compares
success and failure trajectories to detect missing capabilities, which
are summarized as new skills to build a skill handbook that can be
consulted to identify required skills and route the task to the
appropriate agent. Skill management organizes and updates a collection
of skills, including removing redundant skills, pruning low-quality
ones, refining skills as an up-to-date version, and controlling the size
of skill library. For example,
AgentSkillOS <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a> organizes
skills into a capability tree, where the tree nodes are determined by
skill categories and store the skill content. To keep the tree
manageable, only the top-ranking skills are retained. AgentSkillOS
traverses the tree to retrieve skills and caches successful
orchestration plans for reuse. Similarly, SSL
(Scheduling-Structural-Logical) <a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>
converts the original skill text into a graph to better organize its
content, including skill interface signals, operational stages, and
individual actions. Different from implicit RL-trained routing policies,
an interpretable handbook avoids routing collapse, transfers across
orchestrator backbones without retraining, and achieves up to 22.5%
accuracy gains at 700× lower learning cost than RL methods.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>General-domain Benchmarks

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Although general-domain benchmarks were not originally designed for
skill evaluation, they can be readily adapted to assess the performance
of agents that learn and apply skills as below.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### Interactive Agent Environments.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Interactive agent environments evaluate an agent’s ability to perceive
the environment, plan multi-step actions, and execute long-horizon tasks
under current observation states. They are adaptable, using guidance
from skills to assess an agent’s performance and determine skill
quality. <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">ALFWorld</span> <a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">56</a>
aligns text-based interactive household task completion with embodied
ALFRED goals and TextWorld games, requiring agents to navigate rooms,
manipulate objects, and follow natural-language instructions.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">WebShop</span> <a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref">79</a>
simulates online shopping over 1.18M real product listings with 12,087
crowd-sourced instructions, evaluating product search, attribute
comparison, and goal-directed purchasing.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">ScienceWorld</span> <a href="#bib.bib71" class="ltx_ref">71</a>
provides an interactive text environment at the level of an
elementary-school science curriculum, with 30 benchmark tasks (and 7,200
parametric variations) spanning thermodynamics, electrical circuits,
chemistry, and biological processes. <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">WebArena</span> <a href="#bib.bib87" class="ltx_ref">87</a>
offers 812 realistic long-horizon web-based tasks requiring multi-step
browser interaction across four real-world web applications (e-commerce,
social forums, collaborative development, content management).
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AgentBench</span> <a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">35</a>
consolidates eight distinct interactive environments (e.g., operating
system, database, knowledge graph) into a unified evaluation framework
for assessing LLM-as-Agent reasoning and decision-making.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AppWorld</span> <a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>
provides a controllable world of 9 day-to-day apps operable via 457
APIs, with 750 natural-agent tasks for benchmarking interactive coding
agents over stateful application use.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### Code Generation and Software Engineering.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Code-generation and software-engineering benchmarks evaluate functional
correctness, command-line proficiency, and efficiency of program
synthesis. They are relevant to skill evaluation because reusable
engineering knowledge, including algorithmic recipes, debugging
routines, and build-system patterns, is naturally described in the skill
packages, and performance on these benchmarks could indicate whether the
existing skills are useful for improving code generation or solving
software engineering tasks. <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Terminal-Bench</span> <a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">43</a>
provides 89 hard, realistic command-line tasks for evaluating raw agent
harness capability through shell-based interactions and is used by
SkillFlow <a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">HumanEval</span> <a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>
releases 164 hand-written Python programming problems with unit tests to
measure functional correctness of code synthesized from docstrings.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">MBPP</span> <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">3</a>
(Mostly Basic Programming Problems) contains 974 entry-level Python
tasks (374 train / 90 val / 500 test) crowd-sourced to cover programming
fundamentals and standard-library usage. For code efficiency,
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">EffiBench-X</span> <a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">51</a>
is the first large-scale multi-language code efficiency benchmark
covering Python, C++, Java, JavaScript, Ruby, and Go, and serves as the
primary evaluation for
EffiSkill <a href="#bib.bib72" class="ltx_ref">72</a>. The
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">PIE
dataset</span> <a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">57</a> contains over
77K paired slow/fast competitive C++ programming submissions across
1,474 problems and is used by EffiSkill for offline mining of recurring
slow-to-fast transformations.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### Mathematical Reasoning.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Mathematical reasoning benchmarks evaluate multi-step symbolic and
quantitative reasoning with verifiable answers. They are well-suited to
skill evaluation as mathematical solution strategies are reusable across
problems. <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AMC/AIME</span> <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">2</a>
are annual competition-level problem sets ($`\approx`$<!-- -->30
problems per year) from the American Mathematics Competitions and the
American Invitational Mathematics Examination, used as in-distribution
and out-of-distribution mathematical reasoning evaluations.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Omni-MATH</span> <a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">11</a>
provides 4,428 Olympiad-level problems spanning 33 sub-domains, forming
the out-of-distribution suite together with AMC/AIME for
ARISE <a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>, which trains on the
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">DeepScaleR</span> <a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>
dataset of approximately 40K math problem-answer pairs compiled from
AIME, AMC, Omni-MATH, and Still. AIME is also used by
Trace2Skill <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a> as one of its
math reasoning evaluation domains.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### Question Answering and Knowledge-Intensive Tasks.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Search-augmented QA benchmarks evaluate retrieval, multi-hop reasoning,
and tool-augmented information seeking with verifiable answers. They are
central to skill evaluation because skills that encapsulate query
decomposition, evidence selection, and cross-document synthesis can be
measured by improvements on questions whose answers cannot be retrieved
in a single hop.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Single-hop benchmarks include <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Natural Questions
(NQ)</span> <a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>, derived from
307,373 / 7,830 / 7,842 real anonymized Google search queries with
Wikipedia answer annotations; <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">TriviaQA</span> <a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a>,
consisting of over 650K question-answer-evidence triples (95K
author-written QA pairs); and <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">PopQA</span> <a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">42</a>,
14K QA pairs converted from Wikidata triples to probe long-tail entity
knowledge. Multi-hop benchmarks include
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">HotpotQA</span> <a href="#bib.bib78" class="ltx_ref">78</a>
with 113K Wikipedia-based questions and supporting facts;
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">2WikiMultiHopQA</span> <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>,
192,606multi-hop questions combining structured and unstructured
Wikipedia/Wikidata evidence; <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">MuSiQue</span> <a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>,
25K 2-4-hop questions systematically composed from connected single-hop
pairs to enforce genuine multi-hop reasoning; and
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Bamboogle</span> <a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">50</a>,
125 manually constructed 2-hop questions designed to expose the
compositionality gap. Together these seven form the search-augmented
suite used by SkillRL <a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">76</a>.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">WikiTableQuestions</span> <a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>
contains 22,033 complex questions over 2,108 semi-structured Wikipedia
tables requiring compositional semantic parsing, and is used by
Trace2Skill <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a> as an
out-of-distribution evaluation that demonstrates cross-model skill
transfer (up to $`+57.65`$% absolute gain). For agent-style
general-knowledge tasks, <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GAIA (General AI
Assistants)</span> <a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">44</a> provides
466 questions (166 validation / 300 sequestered test) requiring web
search, tools, and multi-step reasoning, and
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2.1.10"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Humanity’s Last Exam
(HLE)</span> <a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">49</a> contributes
2,500 expert-validated questions spanning mathematics, sciences, and
humanities; both are used by
Memento-Skills <a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref">86</a> to evaluate
continual skill-based agent improvement, achieving $`26.2`$% and
$`116.2`$% relative gains, respectively.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### Knowledge, Language, and Instruction-Following.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Knowledge and instruction-following benchmarks evaluate the breadth of
factual knowledge, open-ended dialogue, and adherence to user
instructions. They could be extended to skill evaluation to assess the
capability of skills in improving task analysis, response structuring,
and tone/format adaptation. <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">MMLU</span> <a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>
provides 15,908 multiple-choice questions across 57 subjects spanning
STEM, humanities, social sciences, and professional domains.
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AlpacaEval</span> <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>
comprises 805 instruction prompts with GPT-4-based pairwise win-rate
annotation against a reference model, <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">MT-Bench</span> <a href="#bib.bib83" class="ltx_ref">83</a>
contains 80 multi-turn questions across 8 categories evaluated via
LLM-as-judge, and <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">WildBench</span> <a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">33</a>
consists of 1,024 challenging tasks carefully curated from over one
million real WildChat user-chatbot conversation logs; collectively these
form the instruction-following suite used by
SkillOrchestra <a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref">68</a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px6" class="section ltx_paragraph">

### Memory and Conversational Benchmarks.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1" class="ltx_para">

Memory-centric benchmarks evaluate whether agents can extract,
consolidate, and recall information across long interaction histories or
multi-turn dialogues. They are adapted to validate the performance of
using skills for summarization, indexing, and retrieval over past
experience, which enables agents to operate beyond a single context
window. <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">LoCoMo</span> <a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">41</a>
provides very long dialogues with $`\sim`$<!-- -->300 turns spanning up
to 35 sessions, accompanied by question-answering, summarization, and
multimodal probes; <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">LongMemEval</span> <a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref">75</a>
contributes 500 QA items over long chat histories under both synthetic
and realistic settings; together with <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">HotpotQA</span> <a href="#bib.bib78" class="ltx_ref">78</a>
(multi-hop QA) and <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">ALFWorld</span>, these are used by
MemSkill <a href="#bib.bib82" class="ltx_ref">82</a> to evaluate
learnable memory skills for extracting and consolidating information
across long interaction histories. <span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">StuLife</span> <a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">6</a>
simulates a student’s holistic college journey across three core phases
and ten sub-scenarios in a persistent, stateful campus environment
(1,284 interdependent tasks spanning a full academic year), and is
referenced by AutoSkill <a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a> as a
representative experience-driven lifelong learning benchmark for
evaluating self-evolving agents.

</div>
